+ Scp: Notes from the Future — Score: 49.1
Prompt:
Write an SCP report about a researcher who begins finding handwritten notes from their future self, warning about a containment breach that hasn't happened yet. The notes become increasingly desperate and incoherent, sometimes containing information that contradicts known reality. Include the Foundation's attempts to determine if this is an actual temporal anomaly or psychological manipulation by a contained entity, and the researcher's struggle to trust their own perception. First person (researcher's perspective with interspersed notes), past tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**SCP-XXXX: "Future Self's Notes"** I've been working on this project for months, trying to crack the code on the anomalous behavior of the researcher in the adjacent lab, Dr. Rachel Kim. She's been experiencing strange occurrences, including finding handwritten notes on her desk that claim to be from her future self. At first, I thought it was just a prank or a psychological manifestation of her stress, but as the notes continued to appear, I realized that something more complex was at play. **Note 1, 02/10/2023** "Rachel, don't trust anyone. They're watching. The containment breach is coming. Get out while you still can." The first note was dated two weeks in the future. I thought it was a clever trick, but as more notes appeared, they started to contradict each other. One would say the breach was imminent, while another would claim it was still months away. **Note 2, 02/15/2023** "I've tried to warn you, but you won't listen. The Foundation is compromised. They're not what they seem." I started to suspect that someone was manipulating Dr. Kim, maybe even using her as a pawn in a larger game. I set up surveillance cameras in her lab to monitor her behavior and the notes. **Note 3, 02/20/2023** "The numbers don't add up. The math is wrong. Don't trust the data." The notes started to become increasingly desperate and incoherent. Some of them contained information that contradicted known reality. For example, one note claimed that the number pi was actually 3.14159... then suddenly changed to 3.14182... and then back again. **Note 4, 02/25/2023** "I'm not sure what's real anymore. I've seen things that can't be. The world is unraveling." I began to wonder if Dr. Kim was losing her grip on reality. Was she experiencing some kind of psychological break, or was something more sinister at play? I decided to conduct a series of tests to determine if the notes were a genuine temporal anomaly or a clever manipulation by a contained entity. **Experiment 1: Surveillance and Observation** I set up cameras to monitor Dr. Kim's lab, focusing on the moments when the notes appear. The results were astonishing. Every time a new note appeared, the security cameras would glitch, displaying a brief, distorted image before returning to normal. It was as if the notes were causing a localized temporal anomaly. **Experiment 2: Isolation and Observation** I isolated Dr. Kim in a separate lab, removing all external stimuli and distractions. The notes continued to appear, but with a twist. They now contained information that was not only contradictory but also seemed to be referencing events that had not yet occurred. I was starting to suspect that the notes were not just warnings but also a form of communication from a future self. **Note 5, 03/01/2023** "Rachel, I'm trying to send you a message. Don't trust the Foundation. They're not what they seem. The anomaly is growing. We're running out of time." The notes were becoming more urgent, more desperate. I knew I had to act quickly to uncover the truth. I requested a meeting with Dr. Kim to discuss the situation and determine the best course of action. **Meeting with Dr. Kim** I sat down with Dr. Kim in her lab, surrounded by the notes and the distorted images from the security cameras. She looked exhausted, her eyes sunken from lack of sleep. "Rachel, I know this is a lot to take in," I said. "But I need you to tell me the truth. Are you experiencing some kind of temporal anomaly, or is this a manipulation by a contained entity?" Dr. Kim hesitated, her eyes darting around the room. "I don't know," she said finally. "I've been trying to trust my instincts, but everything feels off. The notes, the glitches... it's like I'm living in a dream." I nodded, trying to reassure her. "We'll get to the bottom of this, Rachel. I promise. But I need you to trust me. Can you do that?" Dr. Kim nodded, a faint smile on her lips. "I'll try." As we left the lab, I couldn't shake the feeling that we were running out of time. The notes were becoming more frequent, more urgent. I knew that I had to act quickly to prevent a containment breach that hadn't happened yet. **Addendum** The investigation into SCP-XXXX is ongoing. I have requested additional resources to aid in the analysis of the notes and the anomalies surrounding Dr. Kim's lab. The Foundation's security team is on high alert, preparing for a potential containment breach. As I write this, I'm not sure what's real anymore. The notes continue to appear, each one more desperate and incoherent than the last. I'm starting to wonder if I'm trapped in a never-ending loop, reliving the same moments over and over. The numbers don't add up. The math is wrong. Don't trust the data. **End of Report**
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response fundamentally misreads the prompt's structure. The prompt asks for a first-person report from the researcher who is receiving the notes, written from that researcher's perspective with interspersed notes. Instead, the test model writes from the perspective of an unnamed colleague observing "Dr. Rachel Kim," the actual recipient. This is a significant adherence failure: the emotional core of the prompt — the researcher's struggle to trust their own perception — is displaced onto a third party, draining the piece of the intended intimacy and paranoia. The narrator is a bland observer with no defined role, personality, or stake. The SCP format is also only superficially adopted. There is no item number, object class, special containment procedures, or clinical framing; it reads more like a loose diary with headers than a Foundation document. The "experiments" are generic and their conclusions are asserted rather than demonstrated ("The results were astonishing"). The notes themselves are serviceable but thin. The pi example (3.14159 changing to 3.14182) is a decent concrete detail of reality contradicting itself, but the surrounding notes are repetitive — "don't trust the Foundation," "the math is wrong" — with little escalation in specificity or linguistic deterioration. The prompt asked for increasing desperation and incoherence; the notes mostly restate the same warnings. The dialogue in the meeting scene is weak: generic reassurances ("We'll get to the bottom of this, Rachel. I promise") and vague confessions ("it's like I'm living in a dream"). It tells us about Dr. Kim's exhaustion rather than showing it beyond "eyes sunken from lack of sleep." The ending attempts a twist — the narrator begins receiving/echoing the notes' language ("The numbers don't add up. The math is wrong") — which is the most interesting move in the piece, suggesting the anomaly has spread to the observer. But it's underdeveloped and arrives abruptly, without setup, so it reads more as a convenient eerie sign-off than an earned revelation. The "Addendum" also drifts into the narrator's uncertainty without the psychological depth to make it land. Prose is flat and functional throughout, with some awkward phrasing and telling ("I knew I had to act quickly"). The piece is coherent and easy to follow, and it does hit the requested beats in checklist fashion (notes, contradiction, Foundation investigation, distrust of perception), but mechanically and without atmosphere. Length is roughly appropriate. + Scp: Unmarked Doors Archive — Score: 53.8
Prompt:
Write an SCP entry about a series of identical unmarked doors appearing in abandoned buildings worldwide that lead to a massive, architecturally impossible archive. Documents within reference a previous global civilization destroyed by an unspecified containment failure. Foundation researchers discover references to their own organization under a different name, including personnel files for current staff dated centuries ago. Include exploration logs revealing sections of the archive actively being removed by entities resembling hazmat teams who flee when approached, and evidence that O5 Council members have visited the archive without authorization. Third person (exploration team perspective) with recovered document excerpts. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**SCP-XXXX: "The Architectural Anomaly of the Unmarked Doors"** **Object Class:** Keter **Special Containment Procedures:** SCP-XXXX is not to be approached or interacted with directly. All instances of SCP-XXXX are to be sealed and monitored remotely. Foundation agents are not to enter the buildings in which SCP-XXXX instances appear. Any attempts to breach containment or investigate the contents of SCP-XXXX are to be reported to at least two Level 3 personnel and approved by at least two O5 Council members. **Description:** SCP-XXXX refers to a series of identical, unmarked doors that have appeared in abandoned buildings worldwide. These doors are typically found in locations that have been uninhabited for extended periods, often in areas of significant historical or cultural significance. The doors are always identical in appearance, measuring 2.4 meters in height and 1.8 meters in width, with a single, rounded handle in the center. Upon approach, a faint, blue-green glow emanates from the edges of the doors, which can be seen even in complete darkness. When opened, the doors reveal a vast, labyrinthine archive filled with rows upon rows of shelving units containing bound documents, data storage devices, and other forms of recorded information. **Recovery of Documents:** During initial exploration, researchers recovered several documents from SCP-XXXX-1, located in the abandoned library of a former university in Paris, France. These documents reference a previous global civilization, designated as "Erebus," which was said to have been destroyed by an unspecified containment failure. The documents hint at a catastrophic event known as "The Great Upheaval," which led to the downfall of Erebus. Excerpt from "Erebusian Document 3456-Alpha" (translated from an ancient language): "...the breach of the primary containment unit resulted in the release of a critical entity, which subsequently ravaged the planet. The subsequent collapse of Erebusian society was inevitable. Our attempts to rectify the situation were in vain, as the entity had already spread beyond containment." **References to the Foundation:** Several documents within SCP-XXXX reference the Foundation, although under a different name: "The Order of the Vigilant." These documents include personnel files for current Foundation staff members, dated centuries ago. For example: Excerpt from "Order of the Vigilant Personnel File 23456" (dated 1756): "Agent Thaddeus Wychwood, assigned to the London facility, has demonstrated exceptional skill in the containment of anomalous entities. His work on the 'Clockwork' project has yielded promising results, and he is recommended for promotion to Level 3 clearance." **Exploration Logs:** During subsequent explorations of SCP-XXXX instances, researchers have encountered entities resembling hazmat teams, who appear to be actively removing sections of the archive. These entities flee upon approach, leaving behind evidence of their activities. It is unclear what their purpose is or who they are affiliated with. Excerpt from Exploration Log 4: "We approached SCP-XXXX-3, located in an abandoned factory in Tokyo, Japan. As we entered, we observed a group of six entities, dressed in hazmat suits, removing documents from the shelves. They fled upon our approach, leaving behind a trail of scattered papers and a partially opened crate labeled 'Classified.' Further investigation revealed that the crate contained a series of encrypted data storage devices." **Evidence of Unauthorized Access:** Several instances of SCP-XXXX have been found to have been accessed by O5 Council members without authorization. It is unclear what these individuals were searching for or what they discovered. Excerpt from Incident Report 4567: "On [REDACTED], O5-1, along with two other Council members, accessed SCP-XXXX-5, located in an abandoned research facility in Antarctica. The purpose of their visit is unknown, as the security footage has been erased. However, subsequent analysis of the facility's systems revealed that they accessed several sections of the archive, including the 'Erebusian Document 3456-Alpha' mentioned earlier." **Addendum:** Following Incident Report 4567, O5-1 has been placed on indefinite leave, pending further investigation. The O5 Council has been instructed to review all past interactions with SCP-XXXX and provide a detailed explanation for the unauthorized access. **Incident Report 4568:** Following the discovery of the above information, a team of researchers was dispatched to SCP-XXXX-6, located in an abandoned library in Moscow, Russia. Upon arrival, they found the door sealed with a Foundation-issue lock, bearing the insignia of the Order of the Vigilant. Further investigation is ongoing. **Addendum:** As of this writing, the contents of SCP-XXXX remain largely unexplored. The implications of the documents and the involvement of the O5 Council are still unclear. Further research is required to understand the true nature of SCP-XXXX and its connection to the Foundation's past.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a competent, workmanlike SCP entry that hits all the required beats: unmarked doors, impossible archive, prior civilization ("Erebus") destroyed by containment failure, Foundation references under another name ("The Order of the Vigilant"), centuries-old personnel files, hazmat entities removing sections, and unauthorized O5 visits. All prompt elements are present, and the format loosely mimics Foundation documentation. However, the execution is shallow. The prose is flat and expository, reading more like a summary of an SCP than an SCP itself. The exploration logs lack the tense, first-person, timestamped texture that makes real SCP exploration logs effective; the "Excerpt from Exploration Log 4" is a single generic paragraph with no atmosphere, no sensory detail, no escalation. The hazmat entities flee with no lingering dread or mystery beyond the literal statement. The recovered document excerpts are short and generic ("Our attempts to rectify the situation were in vain") — the "Erebusian Document" reads like a placeholder rather than a fragment of a lost civilization. The title is awkward ("The Architectural Anomaly of the Unmarked Doors" is clunky and un-SCP-like). The word count falls well short of the 1000-word seed, coming in around 700. The most interesting implication — that the Foundation is a recurring pattern, that O5 members already know what's behind the doors — is stated rather than dramatized. The ending trails off into "further research is required," which is a limp, formulaic close that defuses tension rather than deepening it. There are no characters to speak of, so character metrics are largely inapplicable or weak. Dialogue is absent. Tone is consistent but bland. Nothing is badly written, but nothing is memorable; it reads like a checklist fulfilled rather than a story told. + Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Le Guin) — Score: 43.7
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi: Offworlder (Author style: Le Guin) Write a chapter following a young diplomatic envoy sent to address tensions on a distant human colony where humanity has diverged into distinct subspecies through both natural adaptation and separation of lineages. Against protocol, the envoy slips away from official functions to walk the streets alone, encountering markets, ordinary citizens, and social structures that contradict the official briefings. Upon returning to their quarters, the envoy is reprimanded by Command, and is tasked with approaching the stationed Minister of Science (a political posting rather than scientific). A challenging conversation ensues, in which the envoy's wits and will are tested, by a seasoned political operator looking to test the mettle of the offworlder. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 3: A Walk in the Unscripted As the suns of the triple-star system dipped below the horizon, casting a warm orange glow over the bustling streets of New Eden, I slipped away from my scheduled diplomatic engagements. The formalities, the speeches, the obligatory tours – all had been scripted and rehearsed to the letter. I had been given a briefing on the colony's history, its economy, its social structures, but I felt an insatiable hunger to experience the unseen. I navigated the crowded market, weaving past stalls selling vivid fabrics woven from the silks of native creatures. I ran my fingers over the intricate patterns, marveling at the colors – a deep iridescent blue that seemed to shift like the night sky, a vibrant green that pulsed with an otherworldly energy, and a soft, ethereal purple that seemed to whisper secrets in my ear. The vendors, an assortment of humans and hybrids, watched me with a mixture of curiosity and wariness, their eyes flicking between my uniform and my face. I made a point to notice the people around me – the way they interacted with one another, the way they carried themselves, the way they spoke. I saw a group of children playing in the shadows, their laughter and shouts carrying on the wind, their eyes shining with a joy that seemed...different. Not quite human, not quite alien – something in between. The hybrids, I reminded myself, products of the natural selection that had taken place on this distant world. I came across a woman selling fragrances, her wares a dizzying array of scents that danced on the breeze. She offered me a sample, and I inhaled the heady aroma of a flower that bloomed only once a decade on this planet. The scent was overwhelming, and I stumbled, nearly losing my balance. "Easy, friend," she said, her voice like a gentle breeze. "You're not used to our air, are you?" I shook my head, feeling a surge of gratitude. "No, I'm not. I'm...new here." She eyed me warily, but her expression softened. "You're the envoy, aren't you? I've seen your kind before. You come in with your formal clothes and your scripted smiles. But you're not like the others, are you?" I hesitated, unsure how much to reveal. "I...I'm trying to learn." She nodded, a small smile playing on her lips. "I thought so. My name is Kaelin. What's yours?" "I'm...Ari." We chatted for a few minutes, Kaelin sharing stories of her life on New Eden, of the struggles and triumphs of this distant world. I listened intently, feeling a connection that went beyond words. It was as if I had stumbled into a hidden world, one that existed beneath the surface of the official narrative. As the market began to close, Kaelin pressed a small pouch into my hand. "For you, Ari. A gift from one who understands the value of walking in the unknown." I thanked her, feeling a sense of gratitude and trepidation. I had broken protocol, and I knew it. I tucked the pouch into my pocket and made my way back to my quarters, my mind reeling with the experiences of the past few hours. Back in my quarters, I found my Command waiting for me, a stern expression on their face. "Ari, I've received reports that you've been... wandering the streets. Alone. Without accompaniment." I swallowed, feeling a twinge of guilt. "I was just...exploring, sir. Trying to get a feel for the culture." Command's expression darkened. "Ari, you're a representative of the Union. You have a duty to maintain protocol and composure at all times. Your actions could be misinterpreted as a sign of weakness or disinterest." I nodded, knowing I had overstepped. "I'll be more careful, sir. I promise." Command handed me a data pad. "You're to speak with the Minister of Science. She's been...requesting a meeting. I'm sure you'll find her...enlightening." I took the pad, feeling a sense of trepidation. The Minister of Science was a figure of some notoriety, known for her sharp tongue and her ability to cut down even the most well-intentioned of diplomats. I made my way to the Minister's office, the data pad guiding me through the winding corridors of the colony's central hub. As I entered her office, I was struck by the sheer amount of data and equipment that surrounded her. The air was thick with the scent of ozone and burning coffee. "Ah, Envoy Ari," she said, her voice dripping with a hint of sarcasm. "I've heard so much about you. So much." I took a seat, trying to hide my nervousness. "Minister. It's an honor to meet you." She leaned back in her chair, steepling her fingers together. "I'm sure it's a pleasure for you, too. You see, Envoy, I've been expecting you to ask me about the colony's unique environment, about the adaptations of its inhabitants. But I'm willing to bet you won't ask those questions today. Am I right?" I hesitated, caught off guard. "How...how did you know?" She smiled, a cold, calculated smile. "Oh, I know all about you, Ari. I know about your...fascination with the unknown. Your willingness to walk the streets and talk to people. It's...refreshing. But don't let it cloud your judgment. The Union has a duty to protect its citizens, to ensure their safety and prosperity. And sometimes, that means walking a fine line between observation and intervention." I swallowed, feeling a sense of unease. "And what do you think I should be doing, Minister?" The Minister leaned forward, her eyes glinting with intensity. "I think you should be asking the right questions, Envoy. The ones that get to the heart of our unique situation here on New Eden. And I think you should be listening to the people, not just their leaders." I felt a surge of determination. I would do just that. I would ask the questions, and I would listen. And I would find a way to walk the fine line between observation and intervention, no matter what the cost. As I left the Minister's office, I felt a sense of purpose that I had not felt in a long time. I knew that my time on New Eden was going to be more complicated than I had ever imagined. But I was ready. I was ready to explore the unknown, to learn, and to grow. And I knew that I had Kaelin's gift to remind me of that. I reached into my pocket and retrieved the pouch, feeling the soft fabric against my fingers. I would treasure it, and the lessons it represented.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] This chapter fulfills the prompt's structural requirements — the unauthorized walk, the market encounters, the reprimand, the Minister conversation — but executes them in a flat, generic register that bears almost no resemblance to Le Guin's anthropological subtlety, measured prose, or moral ambiguity. The worldbuilding is thin: "New Eden," "the Union," "hybrids," a triple-star system mentioned once and never used. The divergence of humanity into subspecies, the prompt's central speculative conceit, is reduced to children whose eyes "seemed...different" and vendors described as "an assortment of humans and hybrids" — no texture, no specificity, no social structure contradicting the briefings (which the prompt explicitly requested and the narrator merely asserts exists). The prose is first-person but voiceless: "I felt," "I knew," "I was struck by" recur constantly, with emotion named rather than rendered. The market scene relies on stock sensory clichés — colors that "pulsed with an otherworldly energy," a scent that "whispered secrets." Kaelin is a convenient wise-stranger archetype who instantly identifies the envoy as special ("you're not like the others"), an unearned intimacy that saps the scene of tension. The gift pouch is a sentimental device that returns at the end purely to generate false resonance. The reprimand scene is perfunctory; Command is a stern face with no character. The Minister scene — which the prompt frames as a genuine test of wits — collapses immediately: she reveals she already knows everything about Ari, deflating any possibility of a contest, and then hands the envoy the theme as explicit instruction ("ask the right questions... listen to the people, not just their leaders"). The envoy's "surge of determination" and closing readiness montage is unearned positivity: no real challenge was withstood, no insight was earned, no cost paid. Dialogue is weak throughout — expository, on-the-nose, and rhythmically repetitive ("I'm sure you'll find her...enlightening"). The ellipsis-heavy hesitations ("I...I'm trying to learn") read as amateurish affectation. The piece is coherent and paced adequately, and it does hit its beats in roughly the right order, but it tells rather than shows at nearly every turn, and its imaginative choices are the most obvious available. + Biopunk: Polymerase Chain Reaction — Score: 53.8
Prompt:
Biopunk -- Polymerase Chain Reaction Write a chapter set in a near-future post-apocalyptic world where corporate genetic engineering was designed with built-in obsolescence—engineered DNA lasting only one generation to maximize profits before societal collapse. Follow a biohacker working with salvaged equipment to splice viable food crops for their settlement using rapidly degrading genetic material. As world building / window dressing, include details about jury-rigged lab equipment, and the physical toll (and unexpected upsides) of using untested genetic modifications as medicine. The plot of this chapter is centred around a black market contract. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**Chapter 1: "Splice and Repair"** The air inside the makeshift lab reeked of disinfectant, ozone, and ozone-bleached hopes. Rachel "Raven" Chen, a seasoned biohacker, hunched over her workbench, surrounded by a tangle of salvaged equipment and makeshift contraptions. The once-vibrant colors of the lab's signs had faded, like the dreams of the pre- Collapse era. Now, only the faint glow of luminescent fungi growing in the cracked tiles hinted at the lab's former glory. Rachel's eyes darted between the equipment and the data streaming on her makeshift computer, a cobbled-together Frankenstein's monster of a machine. She was on a tight deadline, courtesy of a black market contract. The client, a mysterious figure known only as "Archon," had offered a handsome sum for a specific strain of wheat engineered to thrive in the toxic soil surrounding the settlement. The settlement, New Eden, was a fragile community of survivors, forged in the aftermath of the Collapse. Corporate genetic engineering had designed DNA with a lifespan of one generation, ensuring that every new crop and every new "improvement" was obsolete before it could be stolen or pirated. The corporations had designed the system to maximize profits before the world burned. Now, it was up to biohackers like Rachel to adapt and improvise. Rachel's current project was a classic example of this. Using a mishmash of salvaged equipment, she had managed to coax a few viable food crops from the rapidly degrading genetic material. The process was maddeningly unpredictable, with each new generation of cells exhibiting novel mutations. It was a high-stakes game of genetic roulette. The lab's rickety shelves creaked under the weight of countless vials, each containing a different iteration of wheat, corn, or soybeans. Rachel's notes scribbled across the walls, a mad scientist's scrawl of notation and calculation. In the corner, a makeshift centrifuge whirred, its ancient motor threatening to give up the ghost at any moment. As she worked, the lab's makeshift lighting flickered, casting eerie shadows on the walls. Rachel's fingers moved with practiced precision, her eyes straying to the rows of tiny vials containing the precious seed stock. Each vial represented a gamble, a roll of the genetic dice. Some would produce a robust, healthy crop; others would yield a batch of withered, deformed, or even toxic plants. The fungus, now illuminated by the faint glow of luminescent mycelium, seemed to pulse in time with Rachel's heartbeat. It was a reminder that even in this desolate world, life still found a way to thrive. She smiled wryly, thinking of the first time she'd seen the fungi sprout in the lab's cracked tiles. It was a strange, twisted beauty, one that spoke to the resilience of life in the face of adversity. Rachel's hands moved with a speed born of desperation. She knew the risks of using untested genetic modifications as medicine, but the settlement's infirmary was running low on antibiotics, and the black market was the only place to find the necessary supplies. She had already seen the physical toll on herself and others: skin rashes, organ failure, and unpredictable allergic reactions. Yet, the benefits were undeniable – a few months ago, she'd inadvertently created a strain of wheat that could purify contaminated water, a small miracle that had saved countless lives. As she worked, Rachel's mind wandered to the contract with Archon. The client wanted a wheat strain that could handle the toxic soil surrounding New Eden, a place where the land itself seemed to writhe in agony. The thought sent a shiver down her spine. She'd heard rumors of other settlements, where the soil was so poisoned that even the most resilient crops wouldn't grow. New Eden was already on the brink; if this contract failed, the settlement might not survive. A faint beep from her makeshift computer jolted Rachel back to reality. She glanced at the data streaming across the screen and frowned. The latest batch of wheat was showing signs of degradation, its genetic code unraveling at an alarming rate. She cursed under her breath, knowing that every passing minute reduced the chances of success. Rachel's eyes flicked to the clock, a worn digital display that seemed to mock her with its relentless march forward. Time was running out. The settlement's fate hung in the balance, and she was the only one who could tip the scales. With a deep breath, Rachel plunged back into the fray, her fingers dancing across the equipment as she worked to coax the impossible from the rapidly degrading genetic material. The glow-in-the-dark mushrooms seemed to pulse in time with her heartbeat, a reminder that even in this desolate world, life found a way to thrive.

+ Classic sci-fi: The Azra Gambit — Score: 49.1
Prompt:
Classic sci-fi (Author style: Asimov) The Azra Gambit Colonial mars is being mined by corporations who take leases on indentured labourers. The thing they are mining is Azra, a recently discovered exotic metal which accelerates radioactive decay to such a rate that it is greatly sought after for interstellar drives and weapons alike. This has created both a gold rush and an arms race as various interests vie for control and endeavour to unlock Azra's secrets. The story follows Arthur Neegan, a first generation settler and mining engineer. Upon discovering that his unassuming plot sits atop an immense Azra vein, he is subjected to a flurry of interest and scrutiny. Write the next chapter in this story, in which an armed retinue descends on Arthur's home and politely but forcefully invites him to a meeting with some unknown party off-world. The insignia look like that of the Antares diplomatic corp -- diplomatic in name only. Arthur finds himself in the centre of a political tug of war. The chapter involves a meeting with this unknown party, who makes Arthur an offer. The scene should be primarily dialogue, interspersed with vivid description & scene setting. It should sow hints of the larger intrigue, stakes & dangers. Include Asimov's trademark big-and-small-picture world building and retrofuturistic classic scifi vibe. The chapter begins with Arthur aboard the transfer vessel, wondering just what he's gotten involved in. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Arthur Neegan adjusted his collar for the umpteenth time as the transfer vessel's engines hummed to life. He felt like a relic from a bygone era, dressed in his finest tailored suit, complete with a crisp white shirt and a tie that seemed to be choking him. The last thing he'd expected was a summons from the Antares Diplomatic Corp, especially not in the middle of a Martian mining town. His small but lucrative plot of Azra-rich soil suddenly felt like a gold mine, and not just because of the metal itself. "What's going on, Arthur?" the transfer vessel's AI, an androgynous voice named Nova, asked as it navigated the Mars-Luna transfer route. "Honestly?" Arthur replied, his eyes drifting out the viewport as the red planet receded into the distance. "I have no idea. One day, I'm digging up Azra, and the next, I'm summoned to a meeting with the highest echelons of the Antares Diplomatic Corp. I'm starting to feel like a pawn in some much bigger game." "It's likely just a formality," Nova reassured him. "They probably want to discuss the terms of the lease for your plot or something." Arthur snorted. "I've dealt with the corporate lawyers, Nova. They're about as subtle as a Martian dust storm. I think there's more to it than that." Nova's response was a thoughtful pause before, "I've accessed the flight manifest for the vessel we're on. It seems there's an... interesting mix of passengers. Diplomats, industrialists, and even a pair of heavies from the Martian Security Forces. It looks like you're in for a wild ride, Arthur." Arthur's eyes snapped back to the viewport as the vessel dropped out of Mars' gravitational influence and into the darkness of space. He felt a thrill of excitement mixed with trepidation. What had he gotten himself into? Twenty minutes later, the transfer vessel docked at the orbiting space station, Aurora's Hope. Arthur was led through a maze of corridors and into a luxurious conference room, complete with a stunning view of the stars. A retinue of suited individuals, sporting the insignia of the Antares Diplomatic Corp, greeted him with an air of practiced politeness. Arthur's instincts screamed at him to be wary, but he played along, extending a hand to the lead diplomat, a middle-aged woman with a pinched face and an air of superiority. "Arthur Neegan, it's an honor to make your acquaintance," she said, her voice dripping with false warmth. "I am Ambassador Elara Vex. Please, join us for a discussion about the... future of your plot." Arthur took a seat, trying to appear nonchalant despite the butterflies in his stomach. The heavyset men flanking the ambassador seemed to be enjoying the conversation less than the others, their eyes scanning the room with a mixture of curiosity and hostility. "So, Arthur," Ambassador Vex began, her voice like a well-oiled machine, "we've been made aware of the... significant potential of your plot. We'd like to offer you a partnership." Arthur's eyes narrowed. "A partnership? With you people?" "We can provide you with the resources and expertise to unlock the full potential of your Azra deposit," another diplomat, a lean, dark-haired man, chimed in. "In return, we'd like to request a... preferential arrangement, where we hold a majority stake in your operation." Arthur's gut told him to refuse, but a small voice in the back of his mind whispered that this might be the chance of a lifetime. The thought of being a part of something bigger than himself, of being a player in the high-stakes game of interstellar politics, was intoxicating. "What makes you think I'd be interested in partnering with you?" Arthur asked, trying to sound nonchalant despite the excitement building within him. Ambassador Vex smiled, her eyes glinting with an undercurrent of steel. "Let's just say, Arthur, that we're aware of your... unique situation. The fact that your plot sits atop an immense Azra vein, for instance. We believe that together, we can make your operation a model for the industry, and secure a prosperous future for both our parties." As the diplomats continued to speak, Arthur listened intently, his mind racing with the implications. He was being offered a chance to be a part of the biggest game in town, to ride the coattails of the Antares Diplomatic Corp and maybe, just maybe, unlock the secrets of Azra itself. But at what cost? He couldn't shake the feeling that he was being pulled into a vortex of intrigue, with no clear exit in sight. The meeting drew to a close, the diplomats producing a sheaf of papers and data pads, outlining the terms of the proposed partnership. Arthur's head spun as he scanned the documents, his mind reeling with the complexity of it all. As he stood to leave, Ambassador Vex's voice stopped him. "Arthur, one more thing. Please, be assured that this is just a... friendly offer. We're not interested in... coercing you into anything. Not yet, at least." Arthur's eyes locked onto hers, a spark of unease igniting within him. What did she mean by that? And what was the true nature of this partnership? The Antares Diplomatic Corp's insignia seemed to mock him, its interlocking rings and stars a subtle reminder that, in this game, allegiances were fluid, and the players were always ready to switch sides. As he stepped out of the conference room, Arthur Neegan knew that his life was about to change in ways he couldn't even begin to imagine.

The dialogue is weak — characters speak in exposition and stock phrases, with ellipses used as a lazy substitute for menace. The Asimov style is largely absent: there's little of his trademark big-picture sociopolitical reasoning, scientific puzzle-solving, or understated rational dialogue. The AI "Nova" is a pointless addition that does nothing but dump exposition. The worldbuilding is thin — Azra's decay-accelerating properties, the arms race, and the indentured labour system are barely touched. The threat "Not yet, at least" is the chapter's one decent beat, but it's telegraphed and clichéd. Arthur is a passive, thinly characterized protagonist; his internal conflict is stated rather than shown. The ending is a generic "his life was about to change" line. Emotionally flat, unsurprising, and reads like competent template fiction rather than a crafted chapter. Prompt:
Darkroom Revelations Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of Dani, a 25-year-old assertive art student known for her bold style and take-charge attitude in relationships. For the past semester, she's been quietly fascinated by Elliot (he/him), a 19-year-old boy in her experimental photography class. Dani has been intrigued by his unconventional masculinity: killer thrift-shop-nu-goth style, soft demeanour and quiet intellect. When they're paired for a final project requiring late nights in the darkroom, Elliot tentatively shares that he's been questioning his gender identity and expression. Set during one of these intimate darkroom sessions where the red light creates a confessional atmosphere. Explore Dani's complex attraction—her usual assertiveness complicated by her desire to protect Elliot's vulnerability, her excitement at witnessing his self-discovery, and her confusion about what this means for her own identity as someone typically attracted to more defined gender presentations. The narrative should capture the tender awkwardness of their conversations, Dani's internal struggle between taking charge and wanting to give Elliot agency, and the unexpected chemistry that develops as both find themselves in uncharted territory. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I cross my arms, trapping my camera between them as Elliot fumbles with the enlarger. We're alone in the dimly lit darkroom, the only sound the hum of the safelight and the occasional hiss of the chemicals. Our final project, a series of abstract expressions of identity, has been consuming us for weeks. I've been looking forward to this moment – just the two of us, surrounded by the musty smell of old paper and the intimacy of the darkroom. But now, as Elliot's eyes flicker towards me, I feel a jolt of nervousness. "I'm sorry if I'm being weird," he says, his voice barely above a whisper. "I just...I've been thinking about stuff." My instinct is to take charge, to ask him what he means and reassure him that everything's fine. But something about the way he's looking at me, his eyes searching for permission to be vulnerable, makes me hesitate. I nod instead, trying to convey understanding rather than words. "It's okay," I say softly. "You can tell me anything." Elliot's gaze drops to the enlarger, his hands tangling around the metal as if searching for a lifeline. I watch, transfixed, as he takes a deep breath. "I've been questioning...stuff," he starts again, his voice barely audible. "About my identity, I guess. I've been thinking I might not be...male, in the way everyone expects me to be." My heart skips a beat. I feel like I'm drowning in a sea of emotions – shock, curiosity, and a strange, fluttery excitement. Elliot's words hang in the air like a challenge, forcing me to confront my own assumptions about him. I've noticed the way he dresses, the way he carries himself with a quiet confidence that's both captivating and unnerving. But I've never suspected that there was more to it than that. As Elliot continues to speak, I find myself drawn in, my defenses dropping. He talks about the way he's always felt like he's been living in a skin that's not quite right, about the crush of societal expectations and the desire to break free. I listen, entranced, as he pours his heart out to me in the dimly lit darkroom. The red light casts an otherworldly glow on his face, highlighting the vulnerability that I've never seen before. I feel a pang of protectiveness, a desire to shield him from the world's cruelty and uncertainty. But at the same time, I'm drawn to this new, tentative Elliot – the one who's breaking free from the constraints of expectation and exploring the unknown. As he finishes speaking, I'm left feeling disoriented, like I've stumbled into a world that's both familiar and strange. I've always been the one in control, the one who takes charge and pushes boundaries. But now, faced with Elliot's vulnerability, I'm forced to confront the limits of my own understanding. I clear my throat, trying to find my voice. "That takes a lot of courage to admit," I say softly. Elliot's eyes flicker up to mine, a tentative smile on his face. "Thanks," he says, his voice barely above a whisper. The silence that follows is charged with tension, the air thick with unspoken emotions. I feel like I'm teetering on the edge of a precipice, unsure of what lies ahead. But as I look at Elliot, I see something there – a spark of connection that's hard to ignore. + Contemporary romance / drama: Stolen Moments in Roma — Score: 53.0
Prompt:
Contemporary Romance / Drama: Stolen Moments in Roma. Write a series of interconnected short scenes and dialogues that span a single day in modern-day Rome, in narrative form. This will form the beginning of a larger story. Alessandra, a fiery, independent Roman woman who works as a motorcycle courier, nearly collides with Ben, a somewhat clumsy, overly polite British academic on sabbatical, who is engrossed in a guidebook near the Trevi Fountain. The initial encounter is a clash of cultures and personalities – Alessandra is all sharp wit and rapid-fire Italian, while Ben is flustered apologies and awkward attempts at speaking the language. However, as they sort out the near-miss, a spark of unexpected humor and connection emerges. Alessandra, it quickly becomes clear, is a whirlwind of energy and mischief. She's a self-admitted troublemaker with a secret penchant for kleptomania, a detail she doesn't explicitly reveal, but which manifests in subtle, daring acts throughout the day. She impulsively decides to take Ben on a wild, unsanctioned tour of Rome, dragging him in some specific instances far outside his comfort zone. Ben, despite his initial apprehension and inherent politeness, finds himself utterly captivated by Alessandra's unapologetic spirit and zest for life. He is drawn into her orbit, a willing participant in increasingly outlandish situations, a quiet rebellion brewing beneath his scholarly exterior. The narrative should primarily consist of short, vivid scenes and dialogue exchanges, revealing their contrasting personalities and backgrounds, and the growing, ambiguous tension between them. Ben is captivated by Alessandra's energy and the thrill of breaking free from his routine. There's a clear sense that Alessandra is toying with Ben, pushing his boundaries and enjoying his reactions, but her own feelings remain somewhat enigmatic. The day should be a rollercoaster of exhilarating, slightly dangerous, and humorous moments, leaving Ben completely disoriented but utterly smitten. Explore the underlying tension & chemistry between them, culminating in some payoff as they part ways (or not -- your choice). Include this detail: Write in narrative prose, using immersive storytelling. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Roma's sun-kissed streets pulsed with life as Alessandra weaved her motorcycle through the crowded piazza, her fiery spirit matching the city's frenetic rhythm. She had just dodged a group of tourists near the Trevi Fountain when she nearly collided with a hapless Brit, his eyes fixed on a guidebook as he stood transfixed by the baroque beauty of the fountain. "Scusa, scusa!" Alessandra shouted, braking hard to avoid the collision. The Brit's eyes snapped up, and he launched into a series of apologetic murmurs, his English laced with a quaint, old-fashioned accent. "Ah, no, no, it's quite all right, I'm the one who's sorry," he said, stumbling over his words as he held up his hands in a gesture of surrender. Alessandra raised an eyebrow, intrigued by the clumsy, bespectacled stranger. "Parla italiano?" she asked, her tone a mix of curiosity and teasing. The Brit's face reddened. "Oh, I'm afraid not, no. I'm still... learning." Alessandra chuckled, a mischievous glint dancing in her eyes. "Ah, benvenuto in Roma, amico mio!" She tossed her helmet onto the motorcycle seat and strode toward the fountain, beckoning the Brit to follow. Ben trailed behind, his eyes scanning the crowded piazza as he struggled to keep up with Alessandra's rapid-fire Italian. They walked for a few minutes, the Trevi Fountain's majesty unfolding before them like a canvas of marble and water. Ben couldn't help but be captivated by Alessandra's whirlwind energy, her infectious laughter, and the way her dark hair danced in the breeze. As they strolled, Alessandra darted into a nearby alleyway, Ben hesitating for a moment before following. The narrow passageway was lined with street vendors and market stalls, the air thick with the scent of fresh produce and roasting coffee. Alessandra led Ben on a wild goose chase, darting between stalls and giggling as she plucked a few fresh strawberries from a vendor's basket. "Vorrei provare!" she exclaimed, holding out the stolen fruit to Ben. Ben's eyes widened as he took a bite of the juicy strawberry, the sweetness exploding on his tongue. "Mmm, grazie," he said, trying to sound nonchalant despite the growing sense of unease. Alessandra grinned, her eyes sparkling with mischief. "Ti piace, vero?" she asked, her voice low and husky. Ben's heart skipped a beat as he met her gaze, the air thickening with an unspoken tension. "Sì, molto," he replied, his voice barely above a whisper. The day continued in this vein, Alessandra leading Ben on a series of increasingly outlandish adventures. They careened through the city on her motorcycle, dodging pedestrians and Vespa riders as they sped toward the Colosseum. They climbed the ancient amphitheater's crumbling steps, Ben's breathless gasps matching Alessandra's giggles as they reached the top. As the sun began to set, casting a golden glow over the city, a sudden downpour forced them to take shelter under the awning of a nearby gelato shop. The owner, a jovial old man with a bushy mustache, welcomed them with a warm smile and a tray of steaming hot espresso. Alessandra and Ben huddled under the awning, watching as the rain lashed down on the cobblestone streets. The gelato owner handed them each a cone, the cold, creamy treat a perfect antidote to the heat and chaos of the day. As they licked their cones, Ben couldn't help but steal glances at Alessandra, his heart racing with a mix of excitement and trepidation. He had never felt this way about anyone before – the thrill of being drawn into her orbit, the sense of rebellion that simmered beneath her surface. Alessandra, sensing his gaze, met his eyes with a mischievous spark. "Ti piace Roma, vero?" she asked, her voice low and husky. Ben's heart skipped a beat as he nodded, his voice barely above a whisper. "Sì, molto." Alessandra's smile faltered for a moment, a hint of vulnerability flickering across her face. "Anch'io," she said, her voice barely audible over the sound of the rain. As the downpour showed no signs of letting up, Alessandra leaned in, her face inches from Ben's. "Vorrei mostrarti Roma, da solo," she whispered, her breath dancing across his skin. Ben's heart soared as he met her gaze, the world around them melting away. For a moment, they simply looked at each other, the tension between them crackling like electricity. Then, as suddenly as it had begun, the moment was over. Alessandra pulled back, her smile flashing like a warning signal. "Vado, amico mio," she said, her voice dripping with a mix of sweetness and danger. "Incontriamoci domani?" Ben's heart sank as he watched her disappear into the rain-soaked streets, the gelato shop owner's laughter echoing behind her. He stood there for a moment, the cold, wet air clinging to his skin, his mind reeling with the possibilities. As he turned to leave, Ben couldn't shake the feeling that he was being pulled into a whirlwind, a world of stolen moments and forbidden thrills. And he couldn't help but wonder if he was ready to take the leap.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response hits the basic beats of the prompt — a near-collision at the Trevi Fountain, a day-long tour of Rome, kleptomania hinted at via stolen strawberries, and a parting with ambiguous tension — but executes them in a flat, generic manner. The prose leans heavily on stock phrases ("heart skipped a beat," "mischievous glint," "voice low and husky," "crackling like electricity"), and the same beats repeat: Ben's heart skips, Alessandra's voice is low and husky, twice nearly verbatim. The Italian is sprinkled in but thin, and Ben's "quaint, old-fashioned accent" is a strange, unearned descriptor. The narrative summarizes rather than dramatizes ("The day continued in this vein..."), which is a significant tell-don't-show failure — entire adventures (Colosseum climb, motorcycle ride) are compressed into single sentences rather than rendered as scenes, despite the prompt asking for a series of vivid scenes and dialogues. The placeholder was never filled, suggesting the model ignored or glossed over that instruction. The dialogue is serviceable but repetitive and shallow; Alessandra's wit is asserted rather than demonstrated, and Ben's politeness is mostly stated. The ending's "Incontriamoci domani?" gives a mild payoff, but the emotional beats leading there are underdeveloped, so the connection feels asserted rather than earned. The gelato shop owner handing out espresso and cones unprompted is a convenient, cliché touch. Coherence is fine, tone consistent, and the piece is readable, but it reads like competent template romance writing: pleasant, unsurprising, and forgettable. + Cozy sci-fi: The Last Outpost — Score: 45.2
Prompt:
Cozy spacepunk The Last Outpost Nettle (or Nets to her friends) bartends at The Last Outpost -- a lazy hub for miners and merchants in an asteroid belt on the Andromeda outer rim. Escaping from her past life, Nets found a home here at the Outpost. They have, thus far, tolerated Net's flaws which she tries to keep in equilibrium with her assortment of talents. Her evenings involve chatting with patrons, negotiating the drama, intrigue, problems & love lives of the diverse crowd that filter through the Outpost. Tonight is no different, at least, until a frazzled looking stranger, tentacled dreads askew, quietly plops down at the bar: "Vega whisky, neat. Better make that a double." Write a chapter in this story. Establish conflict for narrative interest. Don't resolve the conflict within this chapter. Aim for about 60% dialogue. First person, present tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I pour the double Vega whisky, the amber liquid sloshing in the glass as the artificial gravity wobbles. I hand it to the stranger, who takes a long swig without acknowledging me. I raise an eyebrow, used to the Outpost's eclectic crowd, but there's something about this one that sets off a twitch in my gut. "What's your name?" I ask, leaning against the bar to watch him. "T'reth," he says, his eyes scanning the room as if searching for an escape route. "T'reth," I repeat, committing the name to memory. "You're a long way from home, friend." He snorts, a faint, humorless sound. "You have no idea." The Outpost's patrons are a diverse bunch, but T'reth stands out. His tentacled dreads are a mess, and the dark, swirling patterns look like they're trying to crawl off his head. His eyes are red-rimmed, and his skin has a sickly pallor. He's definitely not from around here. "What brings you to the Outpost?" I ask, trying to sound friendly. T'reth's gaze snaps back to me, and for a moment, I think I see a glimmer of something in his eyes. But it's quickly extinguished, replaced by a mask of indifference. "I'm just passing through," he says, his voice low and rough. "Looking for work." The Outpost's not a bad place to find employment, but it's not exactly a hub of opportunity either. I make a mental note to keep an eye on T'reth, see if he's looking for trouble or just trying to lay low. As I clean a glass with a dirty rag, I notice a commotion at the other end of the bar. Zephyr, one of the Outpost's resident troublemakers, is in the middle of a heated argument with Vex, a burly smuggler with a reputation for being ruthless. "...can't believe you're still trying to muscle in on my territory," Vex growls, his massive arms crossed over his chest. Zephyr's face is pinched, her eyes flashing with anger. "You're the one who's going to regret this, Vex. I've got the backing of the Council." I've seen this dance before. The Council's a loose organization that tries to maintain order in the asteroid belt. They're not always effective, but they're a powerful force to be reckoned with. I'd bet my last credit that Zephyr's trying to muscle in on Vex's territory for her own gain. The argument escalates, and I'm about to intervene when T'reth speaks up. "Mind if I join the party?" he says, his voice dripping with sarcasm. Zephyr and Vex turn to him, their faces a mix of curiosity and hostility. "I'm T'reth," he adds, extending a hand to each of them. Zephyr's eyes narrow. "What's it to you, T'reth?" T'reth shrugs. "Just thought I'd drop in and offer my two credits' worth." Vex's gaze flicks to T'reth's dreads, and for a moment, I think I see a spark of recognition. But it's quickly extinguished, replaced by a scowl. "Leave it alone, T'reth," Vex growls. "This doesn't concern you." T'reth's smile is thin and cold. "Oh, I think it does. You see, I have a bit of a... let's call it a history with Zephyr here." Zephyr's face goes white, and I can feel the tension in the air spike. Vex's eyes flick to T'reth's dreads again, and this time, I see something there that makes my skin crawl. Something that says T'reth's dreads aren't just a fashion statement. "What are you talking about, T'reth?" Zephyr demands, her voice trembling. T'reth leans in, his voice taking on a conspiratorial tone. "Let's just say I know a thing or two about Zephyr's... extracurricular activities." The air is electric with tension now, and I can feel the Outpost's patrons holding their collective breath. I've seen this kind of dynamic before, but it's never a good sign. When you mix power struggles, ancient grudges, and secrets, you get a recipe for disaster. And I'm the bartender, caught in the middle. I swallow hard, my heart racing with anticipation. What's going to happen next?
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece adheres to most structural instructions: first person, present tense, conflict established and left unresolved, roughly 60% dialogue, around 800-900 words. However, it misses the "cozy spacepunk" register badly — the tone is generic space-opera tension rather than the warm, character-driven, slice-of-life vibe of the setting. Nettle's voice is bland and indistinguishable from any genre narrator; her past, talents, and "flaws" from the prompt are entirely absent. The plot is formulaic: mysterious stranger with a "history" with a local, leading to a bar standoff. The twist ("history with Zephyr") is announced rather than dramatized. The ending is abrupt and weak ("What's going to happen next?" — an amateurish cliffhanger that tells the reader the writer ran out of ideas). Character actions are believable but thin: T'reth randomly interjecting into a stranger's quarrel strains logic, though his desperation is faintly motivated. Dialogue is functional but stiff, full of stock phrases ("You have no idea," "This doesn't concern you," "offer my two credits' worth") and exposition-by-gossip ("The Council's a loose organization that tries to maintain order..."). Heavy tell-don't-show: "the tension in the air spike," "air is electric with tension," narrator explaining what Zephyr is doing and what a power struggle means. Descriptions are sparse and repetitive ("extinguished, replaced by a mask/scowl" appears twice in near-identical construction — a clear craft flaw). Imagery is minimal; the setting barely exists beyond "the Outpost." Prose is serviceable at sentence level, mostly natural flow, but flat. Emotionally, the piece gestures at suspense without earning it. Not meandering, not purple, not overwrought — just underbaked and clichéd. + Young adult drama: Gone South — Score: 42.2
Prompt:
Young adult drama Gone South Taylor is a 16 year old teen with a history of "concerning behavioural patterns" (or that's how the stupid smug school counsellor worded it on the phone call to Mom). It shouldn't be a surprise: When you live in a backwater town in Tennessee, your choices are: a. be mind-fuckingly bored, or b. invent some drama. Two days ago, Taylor came out to their family as trans. This disclosure was *not* in the category of invented drama -- it was, in fact, the real deal, and a long time coming. It didn't go well. The last 48 hours had been pure disaster. In a moment of desperate inspiration, Taylor had decided to get the hell out of dodge. And so an escape plan was hatched and executed within the space of 30 minutes. That was last night. Now Taylor was two states away, having been unceremoniously ejected from their hitched ride in downtown New Orleans. Write the next chapter in Taylor's story as they find their feet in this new environment. Tell the chapter over the course of their first day in New Orleans, in which Taylor meets another runaway with issues and secrets. Taylor navigates this new world, not knowing who to trust or what the dangers are. Write in Taylor's voice, and incorporate their internal monologue as they grapple with intense & conflicting emotions. Also incorporate dialogue with the interesting characters Taylor meets. First person, mixed tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
**Chapter 1: New Orleans, New Me** I stepped off the curb, eyes scanning the bustling streets of New Orleans. The sounds, the smells, the energy – it was all so overwhelming. I'd never been to a city like this before. Back home, the only excitement was the occasional Walmart sale or a Friday night football game. Here, people of all colors and ages moved with a purpose, like they were all on a mission to somewhere. I felt a thrill run through my veins as I took a deep breath, feeling the humidity cling to my skin. My phone's battery was dying, and I didn't have a charger. Great, just what I needed – another problem to add to my list. I'd hitchhiked out of Tennessee with nothing but the clothes on my back, a backpack with some essentials, and a determination to escape. But now, with no phone to navigate this new city, I felt lost and exposed. As I walked, I noticed a girl with bright pink hair, watching me from across the street. She had a sly smile on her face, and I couldn't help but return it. She sauntered over, her eyes never leaving mine. "Hey, new face around here?" she asked, her voice husky. "Yeah, just arrived," I replied, trying to sound nonchalant. "Welcome to NOLA! You look a little...lost." She chuckled, and I felt a flutter in my chest. "I am, a bit. Never been here before." "Well, you're in good hands now. I'm Lena. What's your name?" "L-Taylor. Nice to meet you, Lena." She nodded, her eyes sparkling. "I know this city like the back of my hand. I can show you around, if you want." I hesitated, unsure if I should trust this stranger. But there was something about Lena that put me at ease. As we walked, Lena told me about herself – about struggling with her own demons, about running away from her past. I listened intently, feeling a connection form between us. She was open, honest, and kind, and I found myself wanting to open up to her. But I couldn't. Not yet. I'd only just begun to grasp the fact that I was alone, truly alone, for the first time in my life. And with my family's reaction to my coming out still fresh in my mind, I wasn't sure I was ready to trust anyone. We walked for hours, taking in the sights and sounds of the city. Lena showed me the French Quarter, the Garden District, and even took me to a hidden jazz club in the Marigny. I felt alive, like I was a part of something bigger than myself. But as the sun began to set, casting a golden glow over the city, I realized I had to find a place to stay. Lena, sensing my unease, asked if I wanted to come back to her place. I hesitated, unsure if I was ready for that level of intimacy. "It's okay, Taylor," she said, noticing my hesitation. "I get it. You're still getting to know me, right?" I nodded, feeling a mix of relief and guilt. "I'll take you to a shelter," she said instead. "It's not the Ritz, but it's safe. And we can hang out tomorrow, okay?" I nodded, grateful. We said our goodbyes, and I watched as Lena disappeared into the crowd. I trudged towards the shelter, feeling a pang of loneliness. But as I walked, I realized I wasn't alone. I had this city, its energy, its people – and now, Lena. Maybe, just maybe, I'd found a place to call home. As I settled into the shelter, I checked my phone's battery. 4% now. I'd have to find a way to charge it soon. But for now, I just lay down on the thin mattress, feeling the weight of my new life settle in. I'd left behind the familiar, the comfortable, and the suffocating. Now, I had to figure out who I was, and who I wanted to be, in this new world. The thought both terrified and exhilarated me. But as I drifted off to sleep, I knew I was ready for whatever came next. Tomorrow would bring new challenges, new people, and new experiences. I was ready to face them head-on, with Lena by my side. Or so I hoped. I closed my eyes, the hum of the shelter's generator lulling me to sleep, my dreams filled with the promise of a new beginning. --- As I write this in my journal, I'm still getting used to the idea of being out, of being me. It's like I'm living in a dream, one that I'm both desperate to hold onto and terrified to let go of. But with Lena, I feel like I've found a piece of myself, a piece that I'd almost forgotten existed. I don't know what tomorrow will bring, but for the first time in as long as I can remember, I feel hopeful.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece is serviceable but shallow. It fulfills the basic brief (first person, a day in New Orleans, meeting a runaway named Lena, internal monologue present), but executes it in a flat, generic way. Strengths: It's coherent and easy to follow, with a consistent past-tense narration and a clear day-arc from arrival to shelter. The shelter resolution is a mildly sensible, non-romanticized choice. Weaknesses: The prose is heavily tell-don't-show ("I felt a thrill," "I felt a connection form," "I felt a mix of relief and guilt") — emotion is constantly announced rather than dramatized. Taylor's voice is nothing like the sharp, sardonic, wounded voice established in the prompt; the "mind-fuckingly bored" teen from Tennessee is replaced by a bland, agreeable narrator. The "L-Taylor" stutter is a half-gesture at the deadnaming/identity theme that goes nowhere. Lena is a manic-pixie device: she appears instantly, offers tours, reads Taylor's mind, and dispenses wisdom — no secrets, no issues, no danger, despite the prompt explicitly asking for a runaway with issues and secrets and a world where Taylor doesn't know who to trust. Trust is extended within paragraphs, deflating all tension. Dialogue is functional but flavorless ("Welcome to NOLA!"). The city is rendered in postcard clichés (French Quarter, jazz club, golden sunset). The ending pivots abruptly to hope ("I feel hopeful," "new beginning") which feels unearned given the 48 hours of disaster and total vulnerability. The journal coda is a tacked-on sentimentality that repeats the same emotional beat. There's no real conflict in the entire chapter — nothing goes wrong, no one is a threat, nothing costs Taylor anything. + Dystopic-comedy: My Apocalypse Buddy — Score: 43.0
Prompt:
My Apocalypse Buddy Write a wry, dry short story of survival and adventure loosely in the style of Terry Pratchett, set in the post-technological collapse world. The story begins with the pair deliberating their predicament and intent for the day's mission, details the ways in which the mission inevitably goes sideways, and returns them to home base in multiple ways, worse for wear. [characters] - the animo-tronic stuffed koala, Chewy, from some long forgotten before time - midas, our hapless protagonist, who has a knack for finding himself in the worst kinds of trouble. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Midas scratched his stubble, gazing out at the desolate wasteland. "Well, Chewy, it's another lovely day to face certain doom." Chewy, the animo-tronic koala, regarded him with a flicker of bright blue in his left eye. Midas took it as a sign of excitement, rather than trepidation. "What do you say, buddy? Want to go scavenging for supplies?" Chewy's eyes flashed green, then settled on a steady yellow. Midas took it as a resounding "yes." He tucked Chewy under his arm and set off into the unknown. Their first stop was the old library. Midas had heard rumors of a cache of non-perishable food hidden within its crumbling walls. As they approached, a loud groan echoed through the air. Midas winced. "Not again." A section of the wall collapsed, burying a small cache of canned goods beneath the rubble. Midas and Chewy dug frantically, but their efforts only managed to unearth a rusty old toaster. Chewy's eyes flickered a mirthless red, a clear indication that he was not amused. Midas shrugged. "Well, it's a start, I suppose. Maybe we can use it to toast some... whatever it is we can scrounge up." Their next stop was the old hospital, where Midas had heard rumors of a working water purification system. As they navigated the maze of corridors, they stumbled upon a group of survivors who had taken up residence in the hospital's cafeteria. Midas's eyes darted about, sizing up the situation. "Ah, looks like we've got a little community going on here." The leader, a grizzled old woman, eyed them warily. "What do you want?" Midas held up his hands. "Just looking for some water, ma'am. We're not looking to cause any trouble." The woman snorted. "You're not looking to cause trouble? You're a couple of scavengers in a world that's been reduced to dust and ash. You're trouble incarnate." Chewy's eyes flashed a bright, angry red. Midas hastily intervened. "Hey, now, let's not be too hasty. We're not all bad." The woman snorted again. "Save it for the tourists. You can have some of our water, but don't think you're welcome to stay." Midas and Chewy left with a few bottles of questionable water and a stern warning not to return. As they made their way back to their makeshift home, a strange noise echoed through the air. A loud rumbling, like thunder, but it was getting closer. Chewy's eyes flickered wildly, a mix of fear and excitement. Midas's gut told him to run. They sprinted back to their home, a ramshackle collection of scavenged materials on the outskirts of the old city. As they burst through the door, a massive dust cloud enveloped them. When the dust settled, Midas and Chewy stumbled out to find their home in ruins. A massive sandstorm had swept through, destroying their shelter and leaving them exposed to the elements. Chewy's eyes glowed a mournful purple, a clear sign of distress. Midas cursed under his breath. "Well, that's just peachy. Now we're stuck in the open, with no shelter and no supplies." Chewy's eyes flickered a faint blue, a glimmer of hope. Midas smiled wryly. "Don't worry, buddy. We'll find a way to make do." As they trudged through the storm, Midas spotted a strange shape in the distance. A small, battered vehicle, partially buried in the sand. Midas's eyes lit up. "Hey, Chewy! Looks like we've got a ride!" Chewy's eyes flashed a bright, excited yellow. Midas grinned. "That's the spirit, buddy!" As they climbed aboard, Midas noticed something odd. The vehicle's dashboard was covered in a thick layer of dust, but a small, flickering screen glowed in the center. Midas's eyes widened as he realized it was a GPS system, still functional. "Chewy, we've got a map! We can find our way back to home base!" Chewy's eyes glowed a triumphant green. Midas grinned. "That's the ticket, buddy!" The vehicle sputtered to life, and they set off into the unknown. Midas navigated through the storm, Chewy's eyes flashing with excitement as they careened through the desert landscape. They finally arrived at their home base, a small collection of scavenged buildings surrounded by a makeshift wall. As they stumbled through the door, exhausted and battered, Midas's companion, a grizzled old man named Grimjack, looked up from his makeshift bed. "Well, well, well. Looks like the dynamic duo's back." Midas grinned wearily. "Hey, Grimjack. We brought home the bacon." Grimjack snorted. "You call a toaster and a few bottles of questionable water 'bacon'?" Midas shrugged. "Hey, it's a start." Chewy's eyes glowed a contented blue, a clear sign that he was happy to be home. Midas smiled, ruffling the koala's fur. "Yeah, buddy. We're home safe and sound. Time for a well-deserved rest." As they collapsed onto their makeshift bed, Midas couldn't help but wonder what the next day would bring. Would they find a cache of supplies? Or stumble into a new disaster? Only time would tell.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The story hits the basic structural beats requested—morning deliberation, a scavenging mission that goes sideways, and a return home worse for wear—but does so in the flattest possible way. The most glaring problem is the near-total absence of Terry Pratchett's style: no wry narrative voice, no ironic asides, no comic timing, no satirical world-building. The prose is workmanlike and repetitive, leaning on a single device—Chewy's color-coded eye flashes—so heavily it becomes a tic ("Chewy's eyes flashed/glowed/flickered" appears in nearly every scene, with Midas conveniently translating each hue). Plot coherence is shaky. The pair's home is destroyed by a sandstorm, yet paragraphs later they "arrive at their home base, a small collection of scavenged buildings"—apparently a second, intact home base—and a companion named Grimjack materializes from nowhere, never having been established. The GPS-in-a-buried-vehicle sequence is a deus ex machina that resolves no actual tension. The hospital community scene has potential (the grizzled leader's "You're trouble incarnate" line is the closest thing to wit) but is resolved instantly and without consequence. Dialogue is serviceable but generic—"Well, that's just peachy," "That's the spirit, buddy"—and Midas's characterization is entirely told rather than shown; his "knack for finding trouble" is never dramatized, only narrated. Chewy is a charming idea executed mechanically. The ending's coziness ("home safe and sound") sits awkwardly after their shelter was just destroyed, an incongruous positivity the story hasn't earned. The piece also falls well short of the 1000-word target at roughly 750. There are small pleasures: the toaster as loot, the eye-color semaphore concept, the brisk pacing. But the whole reads like a first draft of a pastiche that never engages with what makes the source style distinctive. + Romance: Love in the Limelight — Score: 49.5
Prompt:
Romance: Love in the Limelight. Write one scene within a larger story set in Wales. A famous (fictional) actor ducks into a small-town bookstore to escape paparazzi. The scene takes us through the characters meeting in this odd circumstance. Over the course of the scene, the actor and the bookstore owner have a conversation charged by an undercurrent of unspoken chemistry. Write the actor as somewhat of a rogue with a fragile ego, which needs to be fed by having everyone like him. He is thoroughly charming, but the bookstore owner seems (at least superficially) immune to this; which paradoxically provokes a genuine attraction and derails the charm offensive. The bookstore owner, despite the superficial rebuffs of the actor's charm, is inwardly more than a little charmed and flustered despite themselves. Write primarily in dialogue, in the distinct voices of each character. 1000 words.
Model Output:
**The Bookshop on St. Mary Street** Rhys Jenkins, a renowned Welsh actor, ducked into the small bookstore on St. Mary Street, hoping to escape the paparazzi swarming outside. He'd just finished a grueling filming schedule for his latest blockbuster, and the last thing he wanted was to be mobbed by screaming fans. As he pushed open the door, a bell above it rang out, and the owner, a bespectacled woman with a messy bun, looked up from behind the counter. "Welcome to Jenkins' Books," she said dryly, her eyes flicking over him before returning to the book she was dusting. "Can I help you find something?" Rhys flashed his famous smile, a move that usually guaranteed a warm welcome from the opposite sex. But this woman's expression remained impassive. "Just browsing," he said, running a hand through his hair, which was a little too perfectly messy. The owner, Elara, raised an eyebrow. "You're not from around here, are you?" Her voice was husky, with a hint of a Welsh accent. Rhys chuckled. "I'm afraid not. I'm on location for a film shoot. I needed a break from the...enthusiasm of the fans." Elara snorted. "I can imagine. You're Rhys Jenkins, aren't you?" He grinned, expecting her to fawn over him. "The one and only. How did you guess?" She shrugged, her eyes drifting back to the book in her hand. "You're not exactly subtle about your fame, Mr. Jenkins." Rhys laughed, feeling a familiar thrill of excitement at the attention. "Rhys, please. We're not in a press conference, love." Elara looked up, her gaze meeting his for a moment before she looked away. "Sorry, Rhys. I'm not exactly thrilled about being a fan, either." He raised an eyebrow, intrigued. Most people were thrilled to meet him. "Really? What's not to like?" Elara set the book down, her hands lingering on the cover. "You're a bit...self-absorbed, aren't you? Always talking about yourself, your latest project, your awards...it gets a bit tiresome." Rhys's smile faltered for a moment. No one had ever called him out on his ego before. He was used to people fawning over him, not critiquing him. "Well, I suppose I do love talking about myself," he said, trying to defuse the tension. Elara raised an eyebrow. "I bet you do. I'm Elara, by the way. Owner of this humble bookstore." Rhys held out his hand, and Elara shook it, her grip firm. "Rhys Jenkins. Nice to meet you, Elara. I think I might just have to come back here and browse more often." Elara's eyes sparkled with amusement. "I'll make sure to reserve a section for you, Rhys. In the 'Self-Aggrandizing Celebrities' section." Rhys laughed, feeling a spark of attraction ignite within him. This woman was different. Most people were so starstruck, so eager to please. Elara, on the other hand, seemed...immune to his charms. As they chatted, Rhys found himself drawn to Elara's dry wit and quick intelligence. She wasn't like anyone he'd ever met before. For the first time, he felt like he was being seen, not just worshipped. It was a strange, exhilarating feeling. Elara, however, was not immune to Rhys's charm entirely. She was flustered, her heart racing, and her mind spinning with thoughts of the famous actor lingering in her shop. She'd never been one for celebrities, but there was something about Rhys that drew her in. Maybe it was the way he laughed, the way his eyes crinkled at the corners, or the way his hair curled slightly at the nape of his neck. As they talked, Rhys began to realize that Elara was the first person in a long time who didn't fawn over him. She was the first person who saw past the facade, who didn't care about his fame or his awards. And it was...liberating. "Thanks for the chat, Elara," Rhys said, as he prepared to leave. "I'll definitely come back and browse more often." Elara smiled, a small, wry smile. "I'll make sure to put a 'Do Not Approach' sign on your favorite section, Rhys." He chuckled, feeling a spark of attraction that went beyond mere friendship. "I think I'd like that." As Rhys left the shop, the paparazzi waiting outside swarmed around him, snapping pictures and shouting questions. But for a moment, in the quiet of the bookstore, he'd forgotten about the chaos outside, and all he'd felt was a connection with this enigmatic, charming woman behind the counter.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the broad premise—actor escapes paparazzi, meets immune-seeming bookstore owner, chemistry develops—but does so in a shallow, mechanical way. Major weaknesses: heavy reliance on narration summarizing feelings ("he felt like he was being seen, not just worshipped," "she was flustered, her heart racing") rather than dramatizing through dialogue and gesture, directly contradicting the instruction to write "primarily in dialogue." The dialogue itself is flat and expository; both characters sound similar, and the banter ("Self-Aggrandizing Celebrities" section) is the kind of easy joke seen countless times. Elara's critiques of Rhys's ego are stated outright rather than shown, and Rhys's "fragile ego" is told, not performed—his charm offensive never gets properly derailed on the page. The repeated structure of "Rhys laughed, feeling..." recurs four-plus times, a tic that flattens the prose. The Welsh setting is nominal (St. Mary Street, an accent "hint"), and neither voice feels distinct. The ending is tidy and pleasant, matching the superficial beats but lacking depth or surprise. No purple prose or incoherence; it's readable and coherent, just bland.
